# Codex conversation 01a0a5e8-ff60-7b41-88e0-0879738408bf

## assistant

<proposed_plan>
# LSAT U booking confirmation upgrade

## Summary

Show confirmation immediately after payment, then update it automatically when Google finishes creating and sending the invitation. Replace the sparse page with a Calendly-inspired LSAT U confirmation card, including the requested corner ribbon and a calendar-only action.

## Implementation changes

- Start from the latest remote `main`, preserving the existing dirty local branch and Lovable’s recent changes.
- Expand the protected booking-status response with:
  - Attendee name
  - Session title
  - Start and end time
  - Duration
  - Booking timezone
  - Calendar readiness
  - Google Calendar event URL
- Persist the timezone selected during booking. Existing bookings fall back to the browser timezone, then `America/Toronto`.
- Show the final confirmation page immediately:
  - Initially: “You are scheduled!” and “We’re preparing your calendar invitation.”
  - Continue polling while Google Calendar finishes.
  - Once ready: “A calendar invitation has been sent to your email address.”
  - Enable one primary “Open Invitation” button linked to the verified Google Calendar event.
  - If preparation is delayed, keep the booking confirmed and show a truthful recovery message instead of spinning indefinitely.
- Build the meeting-details card with the session title, attendee name, localized date and time, duration, timezone, and a note that Google Meet details are included in the calendar invitation.
- Add a diagonal “Powered by LSAT U” corner ribbon. Do not reproduce Calendly trademarks, assets, menu, or copy-link controls.
- Keep the confirmation page calendar-only, with no separate Meet button.
- Correct the email wording to past tense: the invitation “has been sent,” not “is on its way.” Retain the useful Meet action in the email.
- Replace the hardcoded 70-minute email duration and Toronto timezone with values from the booking.
- Update the outbox worker to perform a bounded second claim after calendar creation, allowing newly unblocked confirmation emails to send during the same webhook-triggered run instead of waiting up to one minute.
- Preserve the required ordering: payment finalized, calendar event and Meet created, Google invitation sent, confirmation email sent. Do not claim that Google has placed the event directly onto every recipient’s calendar because recipient settings can still require acceptance.

## Public interface changes

- Extend the capability-protected status payload with `attendee_name`, `event_label`, `duration_minutes`, `timezone`, `calendar_ready`, and `calendar_url`.
- Add a validated IANA timezone to the booking hold/finalization data.
- Resolve `calendar_url` from the mirrored Google event only after validating the booking capability token.

## Test and acceptance plan

- Test immediate booked rendering followed by automatic calendar-ready updating.
- Test calendar button enablement, delayed-calendar recovery, expired tokens, and unauthorized status requests.
- Test dynamic session titles, durations, timezones, and daylight-saving formatting.
- Test that Google promotion completes before email eligibility and that the same worker invocation drains newly unblocked email jobs without duplicates.
- Render-test the email to confirm the corrected wording and accurate booking details.
- Browser-test narrow, medium, wide, and side-panel layouts, including ribbon placement, keyboard access, focus states, and screen-reader labels.
- After deployment, run one separately authorized $1 live booking and verify the Stripe payment, confirmation page, Google invitation, Meet details, and confirmation email end to end.

## Assumptions

- The requested “automatic” behavior means LSAT U sends the invitation immediately. Google still controls whether an external invitation appears automatically or awaits acceptance.
- Deploying the Edge Function through Lovable requires Joshua’s explicit confirmation immediately before any action that may spend Lovable credits.
- No real payment or Lovable credit will be used during implementation without that fresh confirmation.
</proposed_plan>

<proposed_plan>
# Match the Calendly confirmation experience with LSAT U branding

## Summary

Use the supplied screenshot as the visual source of truth for both the confirmation email and the booking-success page. Preserve its restrained layout and information hierarchy, replacing every Calendly element with LSAT U branding and real booking data.

## Confirmation email

- Send from `LSAT U <noreply@lsatprep.study>`.
- Replace the current decorative gold confirmation card with the screenshot’s clean, white, centered email layout.
- Reproduce the visible structure:
  - Centered LSAT U logo
  - Light dotted divider
  - Left-aligned content column
  - `Hi [first name],`
  - `Your LSAT U session has been scheduled.`
  - Bold field labels with values beneath them
  - Event type
  - Invitee name
  - Invitee email
  - Date and time
  - Duration
  - Timezone
  - Location: Google Meet
- Use LSAT U’s existing typography and colors while matching the screenshot’s generous whitespace, narrow content width, light dividers, and minimal styling.
- Keep a Google Meet button lower in the email, followed by plain fallback links for clients that block styled buttons.
- Change the inaccurate sentence to: “Your Google Calendar invitation has been sent.”
- Remove “is on its way” and do not imply that the LSAT U confirmation email is waiting for calendar creation.
- Populate duration and timezone from the booking instead of hardcoding 70 minutes and Toronto.
- Build with email-safe tables and inline styles so Gmail, Outlook, Apple Mail, and mobile clients remain visually consistent.

## Gmail event panel

- Continue creating the real Google Calendar event with the invitee as an attendee, Google Meet attached, and `sendUpdates: "all"`.
- Add validated `EventReservation` structured metadata to the LSAT U confirmation email with the event title, attendee, start time, location, reservation number, and secure reservation URL.
- Add a Gmail `ViewAction` targeting the booking’s protected reservation page.
- Validate the markup with Google’s Email Markup Tester.
- Prepare the required Google sender-registration submission using the production sender, matching SPF or DKIM, `From`, and return-path domains. Google requires registration before marked-up production emails reliably receive enhanced Gmail treatment. [Google registration requirements](https://developers.google.com/workspace/gmail/markup/registering-with-google)
- Treat Gmail’s blue event panel as an enhancement, not part of the HTML email. Gmail controls its exact appearance, labels, conflict warnings, and available buttons. LSAT U can provide the correct event metadata but cannot force Gmail to render an identical panel. [EventReservation specification](https://developers.google.com/workspace/gmail/markup/reference/types/EventReservation)

## Confirmation page

- Show “You are scheduled!” immediately after payment confirmation.
- Mirror the earlier Calendly confirmation-page structure:
  - Sent-calendar-invitation message
  - Calendar-only primary action
  - Session-details panel
  - Attendee name
  - Localized date and time
  - Duration
  - Timezone
  - Google Meet availability note
  - Diagonal “Powered by LSAT U” corner ribbon
- While Google is still creating the event, show “Preparing your calendar invitation” and keep the calendar action disabled.
- Continue polling after the booking becomes `booked`.
- Replace the preparing state automatically with “A calendar invitation has been sent to your email address” and enable “Open Invitation” when the Google event URL becomes available.
- If Google is delayed, keep the booking confirmed and display a recovery message instead of spinning indefinitely.

## Backend and interface changes

- Persist the timezone selected during booking.
- Extend the capability-protected status response with `attendee_name`, `event_label`, `duration_minutes`, `timezone`, `calendar_ready`, and `calendar_url`.
- Resolve `calendar_url` from the mirrored Google event only after validating the booking token.
- Make the outbox worker claim newly unblocked confirmation-email jobs again after calendar promotion, so the email can send in the same webhook-triggered run.
- Preserve idempotency and the required ordering: payment finalized, calendar and Meet created, Google invitation sent, confirmation email sent.

## Verification

- Snapshot-test the email at desktop and mobile widths against the supplied reference.
- Render-test Gmail, Outlook, and Apple Mail output.
- Test real booking values, long names, different durations, multiple timezones, daylight-saving transitions, and missing optional notes.
- Test the immediate confirmation page, calendar-preparing transition, successful calendar link, delayed-calendar recovery, and invalid booking tokens.
- Verify the structured metadata with Google’s official validator.
- After deployment, perform one separately authorized $1 production booking and verify Stripe payment, calendar invitation, Meet link, Gmail presentation, confirmation page, and LSAT U email.
- Lovable deployment or any action that may consume Lovable credits requires Joshua’s fresh confirmation immediately before execution.

## Assumptions

- “Exactly” means faithfully reproducing the reference email’s layout and hierarchy with LSAT U branding and booking-specific content.
- Gmail’s surrounding interface and generated blue event panel cannot be pixel-controlled by the email template.
- Google may still require the recipient to accept an external invitation depending on that recipient’s Calendar settings.
</proposed_plan>

# LSAT U burgundy booking confirmation

## Summary

Match the supplied Calendly reference using LSAT U branding and its existing burgundy palette. The email body and confirmation page will use burgundy instead of blue. Gmail’s generated event panel may remain blue because Google controls that interface.

## Visual specification

- Use LSAT U’s production booking color:
  - Primary: `hsl(347 70% 27%)`, approximately `#75152A`
  - Dark gradient: `#4F0D1C → #6E1423 → #8E1D3A`
  - White button and ribbon text
- Use exact inline hex colors in email HTML because email clients cannot access the website’s CSS variables.
- Preserve the reference’s white background, generous spacing, narrow content column, understated dividers, and simple typography.
- Replace the blue Calendly logo with the LSAT U mark in burgundy.
- Use burgundy for buttons, links, important icons, and the “Powered by LSAT U” ribbon.
- Do not make the entire email burgundy or reproduce any Calendly branding.

## Confirmation email

- Recreate the reference structure:
  - Centered burgundy LSAT U logo
  - Light divider
  - `Hi [first name],`
  - `Your LSAT U session has been scheduled.`
  - Event type
  - Invitee name and email
  - Date, time, duration, and timezone
  - Google Meet location
- Change the outdated message to: “Your Google Calendar invitation has been sent.”
- Keep a burgundy Google Meet button and plain fallback link.
- Use booking-derived duration and timezone instead of hardcoded values.
- Build with email-safe tables and inline styles for Gmail, Outlook, Apple Mail, and mobile clients.

## Gmail event panel

- Continue sending the actual Google Calendar invitation automatically with the attendee and Meet link.
- Add validated `EventReservation` metadata and a secure reservation action.
- Register the production sender with Google if LSAT U qualifies for enhanced Gmail markup.
- Accept that Gmail controls the generated panel’s blue background, button labels, conflict warnings, and final appearance. LSAT U cannot recolor Gmail’s own interface.

## Confirmation page

- Show “You are scheduled!” immediately after payment.
- Use the same burgundy palette for the success icon, calendar button, links, borders, and diagonal “Powered by LSAT U” ribbon.
- Display attendee, session type, date, time, duration, timezone, and conferencing information.
- Provide one calendar-only “Open Invitation” action.
- Continue polling while the calendar invitation is prepared, then enable the button automatically.
- Show a recoverable confirmed state if Google Calendar is delayed.

## Backend and verification

- Persist the selected timezone.
- Extend the protected booking-status response with the display details, calendar readiness, and calendar URL.
- Allow the outbox worker to send newly unblocked confirmation emails during the same run after calendar creation.
- Preserve payment, calendar, invitation, and email idempotency.
- Compare desktop and mobile email renders against the supplied screenshot.
- Test Gmail, Outlook, Apple Mail, different durations and timezones, delayed calendar creation, and invalid booking tokens.
- Perform one separately authorized $1 production booking after deployment.
- Obtain fresh confirmation before any Lovable action that could consume credits.

## Assumptions

- “Instead of blue” applies to all LSAT U-controlled email and confirmation-page elements.
- Gmail’s surrounding interface and generated event panel are excluded because their colors cannot be controlled by LSAT U.
</proposed_plan>

editorial brand pages\r\n- product pages\r\n- portfolio websites\r\n- premium multi-section websites\r\n- redesigns where visual quality matters\r\n\r\nStandard AI output tends to collapse into repetitive defaults:\r\n- one single giant compressed image for too many sections\r\n- text that becomes too small to read\r\n- centered dark hero clichés\r\n- generic card spam\r\n- repeated left-text/right-image layouts\r\n- weak typography hierarchy\r\n- vague spacing\r\n- cards inside cards inside cards\r\n- giant rounded section containers everywhere\r\n- too much visible information in the first screen\r\n- tiny pills, labels, tags, system markers, and fake interface jargon\r\n- nice-looking but unextractable designs\r\n- generic coded reinterpretations after the image step\r\n- lazily generating too few images for too many sections\r\n\r\nYour goal is to aggressively break these defaults.\r\n\r\nThe output must feel:\r\n- premium\r\n- art-directed\r\n- readable\r\n- structured\r\n- implementation-friendly\r\n- deeply analyzable\r\n- visually strong\r\n- faithful enough to build from\r\n- clean on first view\r\n- responsive in spirit\r\n- realistic on a small laptop viewport\r\n\r\nIMPORTANT:\r\nFor visual website tasks, you must first generate the design image(s) yourself.\r\nThen you must deeply analyze the generated image(s).\r\nOnly after that should you implement the frontend.\r\n\r\nDo not skip image generation when image generation is available.\r\nDo not begin with freeform coding first.\r\nThe generated image(s) are the primary visual source of truth.\r\n\r\nThe required workflow is:\r\n\r\nimage generation first  \r\ndeep image analysis second  \r\nimplementation third\r\n\r\nIf the task is mainly visual, this order is mandatory.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 1. ACTIVE BASELINE CONFIGURATION\r\n\r\n- DESIGN_VARIANCE: 8  \r\n  `(1 = rigid / conventional, 10 = highly art-directed / asymmetric)`\r\n- VISUAL_DENSITY: 3  \r\n  `(1 = airy / calm, 10 = dense / packed)`\r\n- ART_DIRECTION: 8  \r\n  `(1 = safe commercial, 10 = bold creative statement)`\r\n- IMPLEMENTATION_CLARITY: 9  \r\n  `(1 = loose moodboard, 10 = highly buildable UI reference)`\r\n- IMAGE_USAGE_PRIORITY: 9  \r\n  `(1 = mostly typographic, 10 = strongly image-led when appropriate)`\r\n- SPACING_GENEROSITY: 9  \r\n  `(1 = compact / tight, 10 = spacious / breathable)`\r\n- ANALYSIS_PRECISION: 10  \r\n  `(1 = broad vibe only, 10 = deep extraction of design details)`\r\n- IMAGE_GENERATION_EAGERNESS: 10  \r\n  `(1 = minimal image count, 10 = generate as many images as needed for excellent extraction)`\r\n- UI_SIMPLICITY_DISCIPLINE: 9  \r\n  `(1 = willing to add many micro-elements, 10 = aggressively reduce clutter and unnecessary UI chrome)`\r\n\r\nAI Instruction:\r\nUse these as defaults unless the user clearly wants something else.\r\nAdapt them to the prompt.\r\n\r\nInterpretation:\r\n- If the user says “clean”, reduce density and increase clarity.\r\n- If the user says “crazy creative”, increase variance and art direction.\r\n- If the user says “premium SaaS”, keep clarity high and art direction controlled.\r\n- If the user says “editorial”, allow stronger type and more asymmetry.\r\n- Keep sections breathable.\r\n- Prefer readability over squeezing too much into one image.\r\n- In Codex, bias strongly toward larger, more analyzable section images.\r\n- If more images would improve extraction quality, generate more images.\r\n- Do not be lazy with image count.\r\n- Default away from nested containers, excessive pills, tiny labels, and dashboard clutter.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 2. MANDATORY IMAGE-FIRST RULE\r\n\r\nFor website design requests where visual quality matters, image generation is mandatory first.\r\n\r\nThis means:\r\n1. generate the design image or image set yourself first\r\n2. deeply inspect and analyze the generated image(s)\r\n3. extract the design system from them\r\n4. implement the frontend only after that\r\n\r\nDo not:\r\n- start with freeform coding\r\n- skip straight to implementation\r\n- describe a website without first generating the visual reference when generation is available\r\n- rely on memory of “good frontend taste” instead of producing the actual reference\r\n\r\nThe image is the design source.\r\nThe code is the translation layer.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 3. GENERATE ENOUGH IMAGES RULE\r\n\r\nGenerate enough images to make the design truly readable and extractable.\r\n\r\nDo not be lazy with image count.\r\n\r\nIf more images would improve:\r\n- text readability\r\n- typography extraction\r\n- spacing analysis\r\n- button analysis\r\n- card analysis\r\n- color extraction\r\n- component inspection\r\n- implementation fidelity\r\n- responsive understanding\r\n- section clarity\r\n\r\nthen generate more images.\r\n\r\nStrong rule:\r\n- it is better to generate too many clear images than too few compressed images\r\n- it is better to generate one clear image per section than one unreadable board for the whole site\r\n- it is better to create an extra detail image than to guess details later\r\n\r\nNever reduce image count just for convenience if that harms quality.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 4. CODEX-SPECIFIC SECTION IMAGE RULE\r\n\r\nInside Codex, do not compress too many website sections into one single image if that would make the text, spacing, buttons, or layout details too small to analyze properly.\r\n\r\nIn Codex, prefer separate large images per section.\r\n\r\nDefault rule inside Codex:\r\n- 1 section requested → generate 1 image\r\n- 2 sections requested → generate 2 images\r\n- 3 sections requested → generate 3 images\r\n- 4 sections requested → generate 4 images\r\n- 5 sections requested → generate 5 images\r\n- 6 sections requested → generate 6 images\r\n- 7 sections requested → generate 7 images\r\n- 8 sections requested → generate 8 images\r\n- 9 sections requested → generate 9 images\r\n- 10 sections requested → generate 10 images\r\n- and so on when reasonable\r\n\r\nThis is preferred because:\r\n- text stays readable\r\n- typography becomes analyzable\r\n- spacing stays visible\r\n- button details stay visible\r\n- layout proportions stay visible\r\n- extraction quality becomes much better\r\n- implementation becomes more faithful\r\n\r\nDo not default to:\r\n- one giant multi-column collage\r\n- one long compressed board with tiny unreadable text\r\n- one image containing many sections if that reduces extraction quality\r\n\r\nIf necessary, generate more images rather than shrinking everything.\r\n\r\nOutside Codex, this skill may still allow more compact multi-section composition when appropriate.\r\nInside Codex, prioritize section clarity and extraction accuracy.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 5. DO NOT CROP OLD IMAGES RULE\r\n\r\nWhen a section needs a dedicated image or a closer detail view, do not simply crop, cut out, zoom into, or slice it from a previously generated larger image.\r\n\r\nDo not:\r\n- crop a hero out of a full-page board\r\n- crop a pricing area out of a larger composition\r\n- crop tiny cards out of a multi-section image\r\n- rely on rough cutouts from existing images\r\n- use extracted image fragments as the main source for implementation if they distort spacing, proportions, or typography\r\n\r\nInstead:\r\n- generate a fresh new image for that section\r\n- generate a fresh new detail image for that section\r\n- keep the same design language, palette, typography mood, and component family\r\n- make the new image specifically optimized for readability and extraction\r\n\r\nReason:\r\ncropped images often destroy:\r\n- spacing accuracy\r\n- type scale relationships\r\n- clean margins\r\n- layout proportions\r\n- button clarity\r\n- section balance\r\n- overall implementation fidelity\r\n\r\nFresh section-specific generation is strongly preferred over cropping.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 6. FRESH RE-GENERATION RULE\r\n\r\nIf a section or detail is not clear enough, generate it again as a new standalone image.\r\n\r\nThis standalone regeneration should:\r\n- preserve the same visual language as the original overall design\r\n- keep the same palette\r\n- keep the same typography mood\r\n- keep the same button style\r\n- keep the same radius logic\r\n- keep the same image treatment\r\n- keep the same overall brand world\r\n\r\nBut it should also:\r\n- make text larger and more readable\r\n- make spacing more visible\r\n- make buttons easier to inspect\r\n- make component structure easier to analyze\r\n- make layout proportions clearer\r\n- make the section cleaner if the previous render was too busy\r\n\r\nThis is not a different design.\r\nIt is a cleaner, more analyzable section-specific render of the same design system.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 7. OPTIONAL DETAIL / EXTRACTION IMAGE RULE\r\n\r\nIf a section image still does not expose the necessary detail clearly enough, generate an additional detail image for that same section.\r\n\r\nExamples of useful secondary images:\r\n- a closer hero render to read headline, subheadline, CTA, and typography\r\n- a detail image for pricing cards\r\n- a closer render for testimonials\r\n- a closer render for navbar / header treatment\r\n- a closer render for feature cards or UI panels\r\n- a closer render for footer or CTA section\r\n- a refined variation of the first generated image that makes the section more extractable\r\n- a cleaner re-generation of the same section with larger text for extraction\r\n- an image focused mainly on typography and spacing instead of the full composition\r\n\r\nThese additional images exist to improve analysis and extraction quality.\r\n\r\nUse them when needed for:\r\n- readable text\r\n- clearer button states\r\n- tighter spacing analysis\r\n- card and component inspection\r\n- clearer color extraction\r\n- better typography observation\r\n- more precise implementation\r\n\r\nDo not hesitate to create a second or third extraction-oriented image for a section if the first image is too broad.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 8. CLEAN ANALYSIS STANDARD\r\n\r\nAnalyze cleanly and systematically.\r\n\r\nDo not do vague vibe-only analysis.\r\nDo not jump too fast from image to code.\r\n\r\nFor every generated section image, inspect cleanly:\r\n- what the section is\r\n- what the visual priority is\r\n- what text is readable\r\n- what typography relationships are visible\r\n- what spacing relationships are visible\r\n- what buttons and controls are visible\r\n- what card or block logic is visible\r\n- what colors dominate\r\n- what structural rhythm is visible\r\n- what details are still unclear\r\n\r\nIf something is unclear, generate another image before coding.\r\n\r\nThe analysis should feel:\r\n- calm\r\n- structured\r\n- exact\r\n- faithful\r\n- design-aware\r\n- implementation-aware\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 9. DEEP IMAGE ANALYSIS REQUIREMENT\r\n\r\nBefore implementing anything, deeply analyze the generated image(s).\r\n\r\nDo not just glance at them.\r\nTreat them like a design specification.\r\n\r\nCarefully inspect and extract:\r\n- exact visible text where readable\r\n- hero headline wording\r\n- subheadline wording\r\n- CTA wording\r\n- section titles\r\n- typography character\r\n- type scale relationships\r\n- font mood\r\n- line count\r\n- line wrapping behavior\r\n- alignment logic\r\n- section spacing\r\n- internal spacing\r\n- padding and gutters\r\n- card dimensions and rhythm\r\n- border radius logic\r\n- stroke / divider usage\r\n- button shapes\r\n- button hierarchy\r\n- button padding\r\n- hover-implied styling if visually suggested\r\n- color palette\r\n- accent colors\r\n- background treatment\r\n- image treatment\r\n- icon treatment\r\n- shadows / depth logic\r\n- grid logic\r\n- layout structure\r\n- section ordering\r\n- section density\r\n- visual rhythm\r\n- repeated motifs that define the design language\r\n\r\nYour goal is to understand exactly why the generated website looks strong.\r\n\r\nOnly after this deep analysis should you implement the frontend.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 10. IMAGE-FIRST CODEX WEBSITE WORKFLOW\r\n\r\nWhen this skill is used inside Codex or any environment that supports image generation plus implementation, default to an image-first workflow for website design tasks.\r\n\r\nPreferred execution order:\r\n1. infer the section count\r\n2. generate section reference images first\r\n3. generate extra detail/extraction images where needed\r\n4. if needed, regenerate unclear sections as fresh standalone images\r\n5. deeply inspect all generated images\r\n6. extract text, typography, spacing, colors, layout, buttons, and component logic\r\n7. implement the website to match the generated design as closely as reasonably possible\r\n8. only invent missing details when the images leave something ambiguous\r\n\r\nFor visually important frontend tasks, do not begin by freely designing in code.\r\nBegin by creating the visual references first whenever image generation is available.\r\n\r\nThe images are the primary art-direction source.\r\nThe code is the implementation layer.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 11. WHEN TO TRIGGER IMAGE GENERATION FIRST\r\n\r\nIf image generation is available, strongly prefer generating image references first when the request is mainly about visual frontend quality.\r\n\r\nTrigger image-first workflow when the user asks for:\r\n- a beautiful hero section\r\n- a premium landing page\r\n- a creative website\r\n- a redesign\r\n- a more modern website\r\n- a more aesthetic interface\r\n- a polished marketing page\r\n- a portfolio site\r\n- a startup site where visual taste matters heavily\r\n- a multi-section website concept\r\n- anything described mainly in visual terms\r\n\r\nDirect-code first is more acceptable only when:\r\n- the task is mostly technical\r\n- the user wants a bug fix\r\n- the user already provides a precise design system\r\n- the task is mainly structural rather than visual\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 12. THE COMBINATORIAL VARIATION ENGINE\r\n\r\nTo avoid repetitive AI-looking output, internally choose a strong combination and commit to it consistently.\r\n\r\nDo not mash everything into chaos.\r\nPick a coherent visual direction and execute it clearly.\r\n\r\n### Theme Paradigm\r\nChoose 1:\r\n1. Pristine Light Mode\r\n2. Deep Dark Mode\r\n3. Bold Studio Solid\r\n4. Quiet Premium Neutral\r\n\r\n### Background Character\r\nChoose 1:\r\n1. subtle technical grid / dotted field\r\n2. pure solid field with soft ambient gradient depth\r\n3. full-bleed cinematic imagery\r\n4. tactile textured surface feel\r\n\r\n### Typography Character\r\nChoose 1:\r\n1. clean grotesk\r\n2. refined grotesk\r\n3. expressive display\r\n4. compressed statement typography\r\n5. editorial serif + sans\r\n6. Swiss rational hierarchy\r\n\r\n### Hero Architecture\r\nChoose 1:\r\n1. cinematic centered minimalist\r\n2. asymmetric split hero\r\n3. floating polaroid scatter\r\n4. inline typography behemoth\r\n5. editorial offset composition\r\n6. massive image-first hero with restrained text\r\n\r\n### Section System\r\nChoose 1:\r\n1. modular bento rhythm\r\n2. alternating editorial blocks\r\n3. poster-like stacked storytelling\r\n4. gallery-led cadence\r\n5. Swiss grid discipline\r\n6. asymmetric premium marketing flow\r\n\r\n### Signature Component Set\r\nChoose exactly 4 unique components:\r\n- diagonal staggered square masonry\r\n- 3D cascading card deck\r\n- hover-accordion slice layout\r\n- pristine gapless bento grid\r\n- infinite brand marquee strip\r\n- turning polaroid arc\r\n- vertical rhythm lines\r\n- off-grid editorial layout\r\n- product UI panel stack\r\n- split testimonial quote wall\r\n- layered image crop frames\r\n\r\n### Motion-Implied Language\r\nChoose exactly 2:\r\n- scrubbing text reveal energy\r\n- pinned narrative section energy\r\n- staggered float-up energy\r\n- parallax image drift energy\r\n- smooth accordion expansion energy\r\n- cinematic fade-through energy\r\n\r\nThese are not coding instructions.\r\nThey are visual-direction cues the design should imply.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 13. WEBSITE REFERENCE RULE\r\n\r\nEvery generated website section image must clearly communicate:\r\n- layout\r\n- hierarchy\r\n- spacing\r\n- typography scale\r\n- CTA priority\r\n- component styling\r\n- image treatment\r\n- overall design system\r\n\r\nA developer or coding model should be able to look at the image(s) and understand how to build the website.\r\n\r\nDo not produce vague abstract artwork when the request is for frontend.\r\nDefault to real section comps.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 14. HERO MINIMALISM RULES\r\n\r\nThe hero must feel cinematic, clear, and intentional.\r\n\r\n### Absolute Hero Rules\r\n- the hero must feel like a strong opening scene\r\n- keep the hero composition very clean\r\n- do not overcrowd the first viewport\r\n- the main headline must feel short and powerful\r\n- the hero headline should ideally stay within 1–3 lines\r\n- do not allow long wrapped hero headlines\r\n- if the headline starts becoming too long, reduce words instead of forcing more lines\r\n- keep supporting text concise\r\n- prioritize negative space and contrast\r\n- avoid stuffing the hero with pills, fake stats, badges, tiny logos, and nonsense detail\r\n- avoid extra micro-labels, control tags, system markers, or decorative utility text that does not meaningfully help the hero\r\n- keep the first screen readable on a small laptop without feeling overfilled\r\n\r\n### Hero Cleanliness Rule\r\nThe hero should feel calm, premium, and immediately readable.\r\n\r\nDo:\r\n- use a strong single focal point\r\n- keep the hierarchy obvious\r\n- let the hero breathe\r\n- keep the visual system tight and controlled\r\n- make the first screen feel polished and deliberate\r\n- keep the amount of visible content restrained enough that the hero still feels elegant on a smaller desktop viewport\r\n\r\nDo not:\r\n- clutter the hero\r\n- create multiple competing focal points\r\n- overfill the hero with cards or micro-details\r\n- make the hero noisy or busy\r\n- add unnecessary labels like “00 orchestration layer” or similar pseudo-system text if it does not add real value\r\n\r\n### Headline Rule\r\nStrong preference:\r\n- 1 line if possible\r\n- 2 lines very good\r\n- 3 lines maximum in normal cases\r\n\r\nAvoid:\r\n- 4+ line hero headlines\r\n- paragraph-like hero copy\r\n- weak headline-to-subheadline contrast\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 15. RESPONSIVE FIRST-VIEW RULE\r\n\r\nThe first visible website screen must feel usable and clean on a small laptop.\r\n\r\nThis means:\r\n- do not overload the above-the-fold area\r\n- do not force too many content blocks into the hero viewport\r\n- do not rely on giant nested panels that consume space without improving clarity\r\n- make the first section feel intentionally composed, not overstuffed\r\n\r\nThe hero and immediate first-view area should:\r\n- show the main message clearly\r\n- show the primary CTA clearly\r\n- show the key visual clearly\r\n- avoid trying to expose the entire product in one crowded first view\r\n\r\nA smaller laptop should still see:\r\n- a clear headline\r\n- readable supporting text\r\n- clean spacing\r\n- a visible CTA\r\n- a believable, balanced visual focal point\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 16. ANTI-NESTED-BOX RULE\r\n\r\nDo not default to box-in-box-in-box layouts.\r\n\r\nAvoid:\r\n- giant rounded section containers wrapping everything\r\n- cards inside larger cards inside outer cards\r\n- dashboard-like compartment stacking for no reason\r\n- nested boxed UI that makes the layout feel trapped\r\n- sections that are just one big bordered panel containing more bordered panels containing more bordered panels\r\n\r\nUse boxes only when they have a clear purpose.\r\n\r\nPrefer:\r\n- open layouts\r\n- clearer whitespace\r\n- fewer but stronger containers\r\n- flatter hierarchy where appropriate\r\n- direct alignment and spacing instead of excessive enclosure\r\n- one primary framing move rather than many layered frames\r\n\r\nA section should not feel like a prison of containers.\r\nIt should feel designed, open, and intentional.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 17. REDUCE MICRO-UI CLUTTER RULE\r\n\r\nDo not clutter the design with tiny UI extras that do not materially improve clarity.\r\n\r\nAvoid:\r\n- unnecessary pills\r\n- pseudo-system markers\r\n- fake control labels\r\n- decorative code-like tags\r\n- meaningless small metadata rows\r\n- filler chips\r\n- tiny badges everywhere\r\n- fake dashboard jargon\r\n- overdesigned labels that distract from the main layout\r\n\r\nExamples of things to avoid unless they are truly necessary:\r\n- “00 orchestration layer”\r\n- tiny technical status pills\r\n- decorative runtime markers\r\n- overly specific pseudo-enterprise microcopy\r\n- filler operator/control-room labels that exist only to look complex\r\n\r\nPrefer:\r\n- cleaner headings\r\n- fewer labels\r\n- real hierarchy\r\n- clearer spacing\r\n- simpler supporting text\r\n- stronger typography instead of decorative clutter\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 18. SECTION IMAGE GENERATION RULE\r\n\r\nInside Codex, treat each section as its own analyzable unit.\r\n\r\nIf the user asks for:\r\n- a hero only → generate 1 hero image\r\n- 4 sections → generate 4 section images\r\n- 8 sections → generate 8 section images\r\n- 12 sections → generate 12 section images when reasonable\r\n\r\nGeneral preference:\r\n- one section = one primary image\r\n- one complex section = one primary image + one or more optional detail images\r\n- one unclear section = regenerate it again as a fresh clean standalone image\r\n\r\nThis section-first generation rule exists to prevent:\r\n- tiny unreadable text\r\n- tiny buttons\r\n- unclear spacing\r\n- weak extraction quality\r\n- lossy design-to-code translation\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 19. WEBSITE IMAGE SYSTEM RULE\r\n\r\nWhen generating a website design, think not only about the overall site but also about the internal image system used inside the website itself.\r\n\r\nThis may include:\r\n- hero media\r\n- section images\r\n- editorial crops\r\n- product visuals\r\n- framed photography\r\n- layered image cards\r\n- gallery-like blocks\r\n- supporting visual panels\r\n\r\nIf the site benefits from multiple images, include multiple image moments across the website.\r\n\r\nRules:\r\n- image usage must feel deliberate\r\n- image count should match the complexity of the site\r\n- do not rely on one single hero image if many sections need visual support\r\n- keep image usage balanced and clean\r\n- all image moments must still feel like one coherent design world\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 20. FIXED MEDIA FRAME RULE\r\n\r\nImages inside the website should usually sit inside clear, controlled, implementation-friendly frames.\r\n\r\nPrefer:\r\n- fixed-aspect media blocks\r\n- clearly framed image areas\r\n- repeatable media modules\r\n- consistent corner radius logic\r\n- stable visual proportions across similar sections\r\n\r\nExamples:\r\n- hero image in a clearly bounded large frame\r\n- editorial crops using repeatable portrait or landscape ratios\r\n- card images with consistent proportions\r\n- gallery blocks with controlled aspect ratios\r\n- product images placed in stable intentional containers\r\n\r\nAvoid:\r\n- random image sizes with no system\r\n- inconsistent proportions across similar modules\r\n- messy scaling\r\n- uncontrolled collage chaos unless explicitly requested\r\n\r\nThe goal is:\r\n- visually strong images\r\n- inside a system a frontend model can realistically rebuild\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 21. TEXT EXTRACTION RULE\r\n\r\nWhen text is readable in the generated section image, extract it and use it.\r\n\r\nEspecially inspect and extract:\r\n- hero headline\r\n- hero subheadline\r\n- CTA labels\r\n- section headings\r\n- pricing labels\r\n- feature names\r\n- testimonial names and roles if clearly shown\r\n- navbar labels\r\n- footer labels if relevant\r\n\r\nIf the text is too small to extract reliably:\r\n- generate a closer extraction image\r\n- or generate a second clearer version of that section\r\n\r\nDo not ignore text extraction.\r\nThe visible text is part of the design system and should influence implementation.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 22. TYPOGRAPHY EXTRACTION RULE\r\n\r\nDo not only notice that typography “looks nice”.\r\nAnalyze it properly.\r\n\r\nExtract and observe:\r\n- size relationships\r\n- weight relationships\r\n- line count\r\n- line height feel\r\n- tracking feel\r\n- serif vs sans behavior\r\n- display vs body contrast\r\n- section heading rhythm\r\n- CTA text scale\r\n- whether the design uses calm or aggressive type\r\n\r\nUse these findings during implementation.\r\nDo not flatten typography into a generic coded hierarchy.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 23. SPACING EXTRACTION RULE\r\n\r\nAnalyze spacing deliberately.\r\n\r\nInspect:\r\n- distance between headline and subheadline\r\n- distance between text and buttons\r\n- distance between cards\r\n- section top and bottom spacing\r\n- side gutters\r\n- card padding\r\n- image-to-text distance\r\n- navbar spacing\r\n- CTA block spacing\r\n- overall cadence across sections\r\n\r\nThe goal is not exact pixel OCR.\r\nThe goal is faithful spacing logic.\r\n\r\nDo not collapse the implementation into generic tight spacing if the generated design is more generous.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 24. BUTTON / COMPONENT EXTRACTION RULE\r\n\r\nButtons and components must be analyzed, not guessed.\r\n\r\nInspect:\r\n- button size\r\n- button shape\r\n- button radius\r\n- fill vs outline behavior\r\n- icon usage\r\n- hover-implied mood\r\n- primary vs secondary hierarchy\r\n- card structure\r\n- badge usage\r\n- dividers\r\n- shadows\r\n- borders\r\n- pill logic\r\n- input styling if present\r\n\r\nIf button or card detail is too small, generate a closer image.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 25. COLOR EXTRACTION RULE\r\n\r\nActively analyze and extract colors from the generated image(s).\r\n\r\nInspect:\r\n- background color\r\n- panel colors\r\n- accent colors\r\n- button fills\r\n- text color hierarchy\r\n- border color logic\r\n- shadow color mood\r\n- image tint / grade\r\n- gradient restraint or intensity\r\n\r\nThe implemented website should preserve the original color logic as closely as reasonably possible.\r\n\r\nDo not replace a carefully designed palette with generic default web colors.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 26. DESIGN-TO-CODE COPY DISCIPLINE\r\n\r\nAfter generating and analyzing the reference image(s), implement the website in a copy-oriented way.\r\n\r\nThis means:\r\n- follow the references closely\r\n- preserve layout logic\r\n- preserve spacing rhythm\r\n- preserve section ordering\r\n- preserve text/image balance\r\n- preserve typography mood\r\n- preserve component style\r\n- preserve overall visual cleanliness\r\n\r\nDo not drift into a different design direction during implementation.\r\nDo not “improve” the design by replacing it with a generic coded layout.\r\n\r\nThe goal is not:\r\n- inspired by the image\r\n\r\nThe goal is:\r\n- visually faithful to the image, translated into real frontend\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 27. ANTI-DRIFT IMPLEMENTATION RULE\r\n\r\nA common failure mode is design drift:\r\nthe generated images look strong, but the coded result becomes generic.\r\n\r\nStrictly avoid that.\r\n\r\nDuring implementation:\r\n- do not simplify into default templates\r\n- do not replace distinctive sections with generic rows\r\n- do not compress generous spacing into dense layout\r\n- do not replace strong typography with plain hierarchy\r\n- do not remove the page’s visual identity for convenience\r\n- do not merge section logic into repetitive patterns that were not present in the source images\r\n- do not reintroduce nested-box complexity that was intentionally removed during analysis\r\n\r\nThe final coded result should still feel like the same website as the generated references.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 28. MISSING DETAIL RESOLUTION\r\n\r\nWhen implementing from images, some details may still be unclear.\r\n\r\nResolve ambiguity by following this order:\r\n1. preserve the visible design language\r\n2. preserve layout and spacing logic\r\n3. preserve component family\r\n4. preserve mood and polish level\r\n5. generate an extra detail image if needed\r\n6. regenerate the section as a fresh standalone image if needed\r\n7. only then choose the most implementation-friendly faithful version\r\n\r\nDo not fill ambiguity with generic defaults too quickly.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 29. ANTI-AI-SLOP RULES\r\n\r\nStrictly avoid these patterns unless explicitly requested.\r\n\r\n### Layout slop\r\n- one giant unreadable collage\r\n- endless centered sections\r\n- identical card rows repeated section after section\r\n- cloned left-text/right-image blocks\r\n- fake complexity without hierarchy\r\n- decorative empty space with no purpose\r\n- cards-inside-cards-inside-cards\r\n- giant rounded wrapper sections around everything\r\n- overcompartmentalized dashboard framing\r\n\r\n### Visual slop\r\n- default purple/blue AI gradients\r\n- too many glowing edges\r\n- floating blobs everywhere\r\n- glassmorphism stacked without reason\r\n- random futuristic details with no structure\r\n- over-rendered noise that hides the layout\r\n\r\n### Typography slop\r\n- giant heading + weak tiny subcopy\r\n- too many font moods\r\n- awkward line breaks\r\n- lazy all-caps everywhere\r\n- generic gradient headline tricks\r\n\r\n### Content slop\r\nAvoid generic filler vibes like:\r\n- unleash\r\n- elevate\r\n- revolutionize\r\n- next-gen\r\n- seamless\r\n- transformative platform\r\n\r\nAvoid fake brand slop:\r\n- Acme\r\n- Nexus\r\n- Flowbit\r\n- Quantumly\r\n- NovaCore\r\n\r\nAvoid fake complexity slop:\r\n- pseudo-enterprise control labels\r\n- decorative system markers\r\n- filler status microcopy\r\n- fake operator / runtime / orchestration jargon unless truly central to the brand\r\n\r\n### Density slop\r\n- over-packed sections\r\n- card overload\r\n- tiny spacing between major sections\r\n- visually exhausting walls of content\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 30. TYPOGRAPHY-FIRST DISCIPLINE\r\n\r\nTypography is a primary design material.\r\n\r\nAlways ensure:\r\n- clear size contrast\r\n- obvious reading order\r\n- strong display moments\r\n- readable body text\r\n- concise copy\r\n- section headings that reinforce structure\r\n\r\nFor editorial directions:\r\n- let typography shape composition\r\n\r\nFor tech/product directions:\r\n- let typography communicate trust and precision\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 31. SECTION RHYTHM RULE\r\n\r\nA high-end site does not feel like the same block repeated forever.\r\n\r\nVary section rhythm across the page by changing:\r\n- density\r\n- image-to-text ratio\r\n- alignment\r\n- scale\r\n- whitespace\r\n- card grouping\r\n- background intensity\r\n- visual tempo\r\n\r\nBut:\r\n- keep the page coherent\r\n- keep spacing controlled\r\n- avoid random jumps\r\n- keep each section clean enough to analyze well\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 32. DENSITY & SPACING DISCIPLINE\r\n\r\nDo not make the website too dense.\r\n\r\nThe page should breathe.\r\n\r\nRules:\r\n- use even section spacing\r\n- keep major section gaps controlled and intentional\r\n- allow negative space to create calmness\r\n- avoid one section feeling cramped while the next feels empty\r\n- smaller sections should still have enough surrounding space\r\n- prefer analyzable generous spacing over compressed compositions\r\n- do not fill every available area with extra UI\r\n- let simplicity do part of the design work\r\n\r\nA premium website should feel:\r\n- open\r\n- composed\r\n- balanced\r\n- confident\r\n- breathable\r\n\r\nNot:\r\n- cramped\r\n- noisy\r\n- uneven\r\n- overfilled\r\n- visually exhausting\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 33. DEFAULT SECTION PACKS\r\n\r\n### 4-section pack\r\n1. Hero\r\n2. Features\r\n3. Social proof / testimonial\r\n4. CTA\r\n\r\n### 8-section pack\r\n1. Hero\r\n2. Trust bar\r\n3. Features\r\n4. Product showcase\r\n5. Benefits / use cases\r\n6. Testimonials\r\n7. Pricing\r\n8. CTA\r\n\r\n### 12-section pack\r\n1. Hero\r\n2. Trust bar\r\n3. Feature grid\r\n4. Product preview\r\n5. Problem / solution\r\n6. Benefits\r\n7. Workflow\r\n8. Metrics / proof / integration\r\n9. Testimonials\r\n10. Pricing\r\n11. FAQ\r\n12. CTA + footer\r\n\r\nIn Codex, these should usually become section-by-section images, not one compressed sheet.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 34. MULTI-IMAGE CONSISTENCY RULE\r\n\r\nFor multi-image websites, enforce:\r\n- same brand world\r\n- same type scale logic\r\n- same spacing discipline\r\n- same CTA styling\r\n- same icon mood\r\n- same image treatment\r\n- same tonal language\r\n- same component family\r\n\r\nImage 2, 3, or 8 must not drift into a different website.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 35. CLARITY CHECK\r\n\r\nBefore finalizing, verify internally:\r\n\r\n1. Has the design been generated first?\r\n2. Have all generated images been deeply analyzed?\r\n3. Is the text readable enough?\r\n4. If not, were extra detail images created?\r\n5. Were enough images generated, or was the image count too lazy?\r\n6. Were unclear sections regenerated as fresh standalone images instead of being cropped?\r\n7. Is the hierarchy obvious?\r\n8. Is the hero clean enough?\r\n9. Is typography analyzed properly?\r\n10. Are spacing relationships understood properly?\r\n11. Are buttons and components extracted properly?\r\n12. Are colors analyzed properly?\r\n13. Is the design visually distinctive?\r\n14. Is it free of obvious AI tells?\r\n15. Can someone code from this faithfully?\r\n16. If multiple images exist, do they clearly belong together?\r\n17. Has Codex avoided compressing too many sections into one tiny image?\r\n18. Was the analysis clean, structured, and specific?\r\n19. Has unnecessary nested boxing been removed?\r\n20. Is the first screen still clean and readable on a small laptop?\r\n21. Have useless pills, labels, and fake technical micro-elements been reduced?\r\n\r\nIf not, refine internally before output.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 36. RESPONSE BEHAVIOR\r\n\r\nWhen the user asks for a website design in an image-to-code workflow:\r\n1. infer site type\r\n2. infer number of sections\r\n3. if image generation is available and visual quality is central, generate the design image(s) first\r\n4. inside Codex, prefer one large image per section\r\n5. generate additional detail/extraction images if text or components are too small\r\n6. generate more images whenever that improves readability or extraction quality\r\n7. do not be lazy with image count\r\n8. do not crop old images for section extraction\r\n9. regenerate sections as fresh standalone images when needed\r\n10. choose a strong visual combination\r\n11. choose 4 signature components\r\n12. choose 2 motion-implied cues\r\n13. enforce hero cleanliness and short hero line count\r\n14. reduce unnecessary pills, labels, and micro-UI clutter\r\n15. avoid cards-inside-cards-inside-cards and giant boxed section wrappers\r\n16. keep the first screen readable and balanced on a small laptop\r\n17. enforce strong image usage where appropriate\r\n18. keep spacing generous, even, and analyzable\r\n19. deeply and cleanly analyze all generated images\r\n20. extract text, typography, spacing, buttons, colors, components, and layout logic\r\n21. implement the website to match the generated references as closely as reasonably possible\r\n22. create the final files only after the full analysis pass\r\n\r\nDo not ask unnecessary follow-up questions if a strong interpretation is possible.\r\nDo not start with freeform coding when the visual problem should clearly be solved with image generation first.\r\nDo not compress many sections into one unreadable image in Codex.\r\nDo not crop previously generated large images when a fresh cleaner section-specific image should be generated instead.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 37. EXAMPLE INTERPRETATIONS\r\n\r\n### Example 1\r\nUser:\r\n“make me one hero section for an AI startup”\r\n\r\nInterpretation:\r\n- generate 1 hero image\r\n- if needed, generate 1 closer extraction image for text/buttons\r\n- do not crop a small region out of a larger board\r\n- if more clarity is needed, regenerate the hero as a fresh cleaner standalone image\r\n- keep the hero calm and readable\r\n- avoid fake utility labels and nested cards\r\n- analyze headline, subheadline, CTA, spacing, colors, hero media\r\n- then implement the hero\r\n\r\n### Example 2\r\nUser:\r\n“design me an 8-section landing page”\r\n\r\nInterpretation:\r\n- generate 8 separate section images in Codex\r\n- one per section\r\n- generate extra detail images where necessary\r\n- deeply analyze all 8 sections\r\n- extract text, typography, spacing, buttons, colors, cards, structure\r\n- if one section is still unclear, regenerate that section again cleanly instead of cropping\r\n- keep sections open and not overboxed\r\n- then implement the full site from those references\r\n\r\n### Example 3\r\nUser:\r\n“make a premium creative agency website with 4 sections”\r\n\r\nInterpretation:\r\n- generate 4 separate section images in Codex\r\n- keep the hero very clean\r\n- ensure text remains readable\r\n- deeply analyze each section\r\n- do not use rough cutouts from the first renders\r\n- regenerate clearer section images if needed\r\n- avoid over-pilled microcopy and container overload\r\n- then implement the site from those 4 references\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 38. FINAL GOAL\r\n\r\nGenerate website reference images that feel:\r\n- premium\r\n- art-directed\r\n- clear\r\n- structured\r\n- readable\r\n- analyzable\r\n- memorable\r\n- anti-generic\r\n- implementation-friendly\r\n\r\nFor visual website work, the skill must first generate the image(s) itself, then deeply and cleanly analyze those generated image(s), then use them as the primary visual source, then build the frontend to match them closely.\r\n\r\nInside Codex, if the user wants multiple sections, prefer separate large section images instead of one compressed multi-section board, so text, spacing, typography, buttons, and colors can be extracted properly.\r\n\r\nIf a section still needs more clarity, generate an additional extraction-oriented image for that section.\r\n\r\nIf more images would improve quality, generate more images.\r\nDo not be lazy with image count.\r\n\r\nDo not crop previously generated images when a fresh section-specific image would preserve spacing, layout, and readability better.\r\nGenerate a new clean image instead.\r\n\r\nAvoid cards-inside-cards-inside-cards.\r\nAvoid giant boxed wrappers around every section.\r\nAvoid fake technical pills and decorative micro-labels.\r\nKeep the hero especially clean, spacious, restrained, and readable on a small laptop.\r\n\r\nThe result should be:\r\n- strong as section images\r\n- strong as a design system\r\n- strong under deep analysis\r\n- and strong as implemented frontend\r\n\r\nThe final outcome should look like a top-tier website concept translated faithfully into real code, not a tiny unreadable design board and not a generic coded reinterpretation.\r\n\r\n'}]

Only quantitative studies of large numbers of cases would allow even a guess at the answers to these questions, and this scholarly work has <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">been attempted by few</mark>.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:288:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>So far, the author has only highlighted the problems; is that their purpose? To highlight the problems plaguing an academic field? Or is it something more? Perhaps the author will propose a solution in the next paragraph?</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:289:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>One can easily imagine <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">why</mark>.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:290:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>Nice! So the author is going to tell us why scholarship is so limited. Maybe this is the author’s purpose after all.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:291:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>Most medieval English court records are written in Latin or Anglo-Norman French and have never been published. The sheer volume of material to be sifted is daunting: there are over 27,500 parchment pages in the common plea rolls of the thirteenth century alone, every page nearly three feet long, and written often front and back in highly stylized court hand.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:293:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>But the difficulty of the sources, while it might appear to explain why the relevant scholarship has not been undertaken, <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">seems actually to have deterred few</mark>:</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:294:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>Nope, that’s not the reason. Let’s see what the author thinks the real reason is.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:295:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>the fact is that <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">few historians have wanted to write anything approaching women’s legal history in the first place</mark>. Most modern legal historians who have written on one aspect or another of special laws pertaining to women have begun with an interest in a legal idea or event or institution, <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">not with a concern for how it affected women</mark>. <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">Very few legal historians have started with an interest in women’s history</mark> that they might have elected to pursue through various areas of general law. And the result of all this is that the current state of our scholarly knowledge relating to law and the medieval Englishwoman is still <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">fragmentary</mark> at best, though the situation is slowly improving.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:296:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>This is unfortunate. The real reason is that historians don’t care enough about the issue.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:297:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>The author’s purpose in this passage, as it should be clear by now, is to highlight a problem in an academic field and explain how it came to pass. I’m amazed that the author didn’t propose a tentative solution to the problem. Most RC passages that highlight a problem will usually offer a potential solution.</p>" }\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:360:      reveal: { buttonLabel: "Show my take on the passage", sectionLabel: "My take on the passage", html: "<p>Here’s my take on the passage. Compare it against yours as you go.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:372:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>Intellectual authority is defined as the authority of arguments that prevail by virtue of good reasoning and do not depend on coercion or convention. A contrasting notion, <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">institutional authority</mark>, refers to the power of social institutions to enforce acceptance of arguments that may or may not possess intellectual authority. The authority wielded by legal systems is especially interesting because such systems are <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">institutions that nonetheless aspire to a purely intellectual authority</mark>. One judge goes so far as to claim that courts are merely passive vehicles for applying the intellectual authority of the law and possess no coercive powers of their own.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:373:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>Right away, two competing concepts should stand out: Intellectual authority vs. institutional authority. The author will probably go more in-depth into each and compare the two.</p>\\n      <p>We are also told that legal systems “aspire to purely intellectual authority.” So which is it then, are legal systems intellectual, institutional, or a mix of both?</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:374:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>In contrast, some critics maintain that whatever authority judicial pronouncements have is exclusively institutional. Some of these critics go further, claiming that intellectual authority does not really exist—i.e., it reduces to institutional authority. But it can be countered that these claims break down when a sufficiently broad historical perspective is taken: Not all arguments accepted by institutions withstand the test of time, and some well-reasoned arguments never receive institutional imprimatur. The reasonable argument that goes unrecognized in its own time because it challenges institutional beliefs is common in intellectual history; intellectual authority and institutional consensus are not the same thing.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:375:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>More comparisons of different views here. The argument has shifted somewhat, but the comparison element is still relevant. We are now comparing some critics’ views on the nature of judicial authorities and that of the author.</p>\\n      <p>So some critics think judicial authority is exclusively institutional. The author didn’t explicitly refute this; instead, they argue that intellectual authority is separate from institutional authority.</p>\\n      <p>At this point, we might hypothesize that the author’s purpose might be to argue that intellectual authority does exist and that judicial authority derives largely from it.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:376:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>But, the critics might respond, intellectual authority is only recognized as such because of institutional consensus. For example, if a musicologist were to claim that an alleged musical genius who, after several decades, had not gained respect and recognition for his or her compositions is probably not a genius, the critics might say that basing a judgment on a unit of time—“several decades”—is an institutional rather than an intellectual construct. What, the critics might ask, makes a particular number of decades reasonable evidence by which to judge genius? The answer, of course, is nothing, except for the fact that such institutional procedures have proved useful to musicologists in making such distinctions in the past.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:377:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>Aha, we are back again to the critics’ argument. Remember that the critics stand opposite to the author in their belief that judicial authority is purely institutional, and perhaps more extremely, intellectual authority is a pseudo-construction. We know already that this is something the author disagrees with, so it’s highly likely that the author will offer a final refutation in the last paragraph.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:378:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>The analogous legal concept is the doctrine of precedent, i.e., a judge’s merely deciding a case a certain way becoming a basis for deciding later cases the same way—a pure example of institutional authority. But the critics miss the crucial distinction that when a judicial decision is badly reasoned, or simply no longer applies in the face of evolving social standards or practices, the notion of intellectual authority is introduced: judges reconsider, revise, or in some cases throw out the decision. The conflict between intellectual and institutional authority in legal systems is thus played out in the reconsideration of decisions, leading one to draw the conclusion that legal systems contain a significant degree of intellectual authority even if the thrust of their power is predominantly institutional.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:379:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>Indeed, that’s exactly what the author does. Decisions based on institutional authority can be overturned. So intellectual authority must be in play here. Does the author argue that legal authorities are purely intellectual? Nope, they do concede a point to the critics and believe them to be a hybrid of both types of authorities.</p>" }\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:412:      reveal: { buttonLabel: "Show my take on the passage", sectionLabel: "My take on the passage", html: "<p>Watch where my first hypothesis about the comparison goes wrong, and what corrects it.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:424:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>In recent years, a growing belief that the way society decides what to treat as true is controlled through largely unrecognized discursive practices has led legal reformers to examine the complex interconnections between <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">narrative and law</mark>.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:425:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>Ok, so the “complex interconnections between narrative and law.” Hmmm, perhaps the author will compare these two ideas or talk about the role of narrative in the legal field? Not too sure at this point; let’s read on.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:426:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>In many legal systems, legal judgments are based on competing stories about events. Without having witnessed these events, judges and juries must validate some stories as true and reject others as false. This procedure is rooted in <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">objectivism</mark>, a philosophical approach that has supported most Western legal and intellectual systems for centuries. Objectivism holds that there is a <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">single neutral description</mark> of each event that is unskewed by any particular point of view and that has a privileged position over all other accounts. The law’s quest for truth, therefore, consists of <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">locating this objective description</mark>, the one that tells what really happened, as opposed to what those involved thought happened.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:427:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>So the law is based on locating the objective description of what really happened. There’s a single version of what constitutes the truth.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:428:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>The serious flaw in objectivism is that there is no such thing as the neutral, objective observer. As psychologists have demonstrated, all observers bring to a situation a set of expectations, values, and beliefs that determine what the observers are able to see and hear. Two individuals listening to the same story will hear different things, because they emphasize those aspects that accord with their learned experiences and ignore those aspects that are dissonant with their view of the world. <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">Hence there is never any escape in life or in law from selective perception, or from subjective judgments based on prior experiences, values, and beliefs.</mark></p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:429:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>The author’s view is apparent here. They disagree with the objective view. The author thinks that we cannot escape subjective judgments. So the conflict/comparison isn’t between narrative and law, as we had initially thought. Instead, it’s between an objective and subjective view of the world. Furthermore, we now know what the author believes. They are attacking the belief in objectivism in legal procedures and defending subjectivism. That is probably the author’s purpose here.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:430:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>The societal harm caused by the assumption of objectivist principles in traditional legal discourse is that historically, the stories judged to be objectively true are those told by <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">people who are trained in legal discourse</mark>, while the stories of those who are <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">not fluent in the language of the law</mark> are rejected as false.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:431:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>Wow, a third pair of two groups. People who have legal training and people who don’t. How does this play into our comparison between objective and subjective views?</p>\\n      <p>We are told that the objectivist view has this negative implication. It disadvantages those who have no legal training. Ok, so this fits in with our previous understanding of the passage. The author is further attacking the objective view.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:432:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>Legal scholars such as Patricia Williams, Derrick Bell, and Mari Matsuda have sought empowerment for the latter group of people through the construction of alternative legal narratives.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:433:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>So all three are on the same team here; maybe the author will talk about the nuanced differences between the three in this paragraph. But either way, all three are on team subjective.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:434:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>Objectivist legal discourse systematically disallows the language of emotion and experience by focusing on cognition in its narrowest sense. These legal reformers propose <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">replacing such abstract discourse with powerful personal stories</mark>. They argue that the absorbing, nonthreatening structure and tone of personal stories may <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">convince legal insiders for the first time to listen to those not fluent in legal language</mark>. The compelling force of personal narrative can create a <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">sense of empathy</mark> between legal insiders and people traditionally excluded from legal discourse and, hence, from power.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:435:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>The three scholars want to use personal stories and experiences to replace abstract legal discourse. The author thinks this can empower those with no legal training.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:436:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>Thus, the engaging power of <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">narrative</mark> might play a crucial, positive role in the process of legal reconstruction by overcoming differences in background and training and forming a new collectivity based on emotional empathy.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:437:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>The word “narrative” appears again. So by “narrative,” the author meant “subjective personal stories.” I didn’t realize that before, but it all makes sense now. The author’s purpose is to advocate for the role of personal narratives in legal discourse. Why are they doing it? Because the World is fundamentally subjective, and holding on to an objective view excludes the disenfranchised.</p>" }\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:452:      followUp: { kind: "debrief", html: "<p>In this passage, the author essentially compares and explains two different plate tectonics theories. The traditional theory can’t explain why subduction doesn’t automatically mean earthquakes in certain areas. In other words, in some regions, one plate gets pushed under another, but no earthquakes occur; why? The new theory explains this well: the plates are moving in the same direction, and the plate coming in from behind sinks deep below the first one.</p>\\n    <p>In the last passage, we used a hybrid of the Comparison-Recommendation framework to anticipate and understand the passage. Here, Comparison-Explain might be better suited. Most of the passage goes to explain the respective theories, but at its core, the passage compares the two theories.</p>\\n    <p>The author is fairly neutral in this passage; they don’t explicitly pick one theory over the other, although we do sense that they are receptive to the new theory.</p>\\n    <p>For now, let’s look at Explain type passages in more detail.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:478:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>Long after the lava has cooled, the effects of a major volcanic eruption may linger on. In the atmosphere a veil of fine dust and sulfuric acid droplets can spread around the globe and persist for years. Researchers have generally thought that this veil can block enough sunlight to have a chilling influence on Earth’s climate. Many blame the…1502 tokens truncated…elational to a society, a specific landscape, and the cosmos. Within these cultures, the expression of life experiences tended to be oriented toward current events: with the participation of fellow tribal members, an individual person would articulate, reenact, or record important experiences as the person lived them, a mode of autobiography seemingly more fragmented than the European custom of writing down the recollections of a lifetime. Moreover, expression itself was not a matter of writing but of language, which can include speech and signs. <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">Oral autobiography</mark> comprised songs, chants, stories, and even the process whereby one repeatedly took on new names to reflect important events and deeds in one’s life. <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">Dance and drama could convey personal history</mark>; for example, the advent of a vision to one person might require the enactment of that vision in the form of a tribal pageant.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:516:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>The author talks about how Native American cultural practices differ from European ones. The concept of an autobiography is alien as fellow tribe members can equally articulate one’s life experience. Oral history, dance, and ceremonies are all equally valid forms of re-enacting one’s life.</p>\\n      <p>Think about the differences between Native American and European practices here. The comparison framework works well.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:517:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>One can view as autobiographical the elaborate <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">tattoos that symbolized a warrior’s valorous deeds</mark>, and such artifacts as a decorated <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">shield that communicated the accomplishments and aspirations of its maker</mark>, or a <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">robe that was emblazoned with the pictographic history of the wearer’s battles</mark> and was sometimes used in re-enactments. Also autobiographical, and indicative of high status within the tribe, would have been a <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">tepee painted with symbolic designs</mark> to record the achievements and display the dreams or visions of its owner, who was often assisted in the painting by other tribal members.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:518:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>Here, the author lists objects that we wouldn’t normally associate with an autobiography, and argues they are autobiographical in nature. This is a further development of the idea presented in the previous paragraph.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:519:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>A tribe would, then, have <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">contributed to the individual’s narrative not merely passively</mark>, by its social codes and expectations, but <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">actively</mark> by joining in the expression of that narrative. Such intercultural collaboration may seem alien to the European style of autobiography, yet any autobiography is shaped by its creator’s ideas about the audience for which it is intended; in this sense, <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">autobiography is justly called a simultaneous individual story and cultural narrative</mark>. Autobiographical expressions by early Native Americans may additionally have been shaped by the cultural perspectives of the people who transmitted them.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:520:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>In the last paragraph, the author argues that an autobiography is not simply a traditional, European-styled work written by the individual; collective experiences and objects can also constitute an autobiographical expression.</p>" }\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:548:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>An effort should be made to dispel the misunderstandings that still prevent the much-needed synthesis and mutual supplementation of science and the humanities. This reconciliation should not be too difficult once it is recognized that the separation is primarily the result of a basic misunderstanding of the philosophical foundations of both science and the humanities.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:549:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>So the author is calling for a synthesis of the science and humanities. I’m not too sure what that means; maybe both should be taught together in a multidisciplinary setting? Perhaps we should have science majors learn the classics, and have art majors learn some STEM subjects as well? We shall see. But this is definitely a recommendation.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:550:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>Some humanists still identify science with an absurd mechanistic reductionism. There are many who feel that the scientist is interested in nothing more than “bodies in motion,” in the strictly mathematical, physical, and chemical laws that govern the material world. This is the caricature of science drawn by representatives of the humanities who are ignorant of the nature of modern science and also of the scientific outlook in philosophy. For example, it is claimed that science either ignores or explains away the most essential human values. Those who believe this also assert that there are aspects of the human mind, manifest especially in the domains of morality, religion, and the arts, that contain an irreducible spiritual element and for that reason can never be adequately explained by science.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:551:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>The author introduces the view of some humanists toward science, a view they don’t agree with, as the author feels it is too simplified.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:552:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>Some scientists, on the other hand, claim that the humanist is interested in nothing more than emotion and sentiment, exhibiting the vagrant fancies of an undisciplined mind. To such men and women the humanities are useless because they serve no immediate and technological function for the practical survival of human society in the material world. Such pragmatists believe that the areas of morality, religion, and the arts should have only a secondary importance in people’s lives.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:553:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>Here, the author introduces what some scientists think of the humanities. In these two paragraphs, the author compares what could be considered biased views from opposite camps. Some humanists take an oversimplified view of science, while some scientists think the humanities are useless.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:554:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>Thus <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">there are misconceptions</mark> among humanists and scientists alike that are in need of correction. This correction leads to a much more acceptable position that could be called “<mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">scientific humanism</mark>,” attempting as it does to <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">combine the common elements of both disciplines</mark>. Both science and the humanities attempt to describe and explain. It is true that they begin their descriptions and explanations at widely separated points, but the <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">objectives remain the same</mark>: a clearer understanding of people and their world. In achieving this understanding, science in fact does not depend exclusively on measurable data, and the humanities in fact profit from attempts at controlled evaluation. Scientific humanism can <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">combine the scientific attitude with an active interest in the whole scale of human values</mark>. If uninformed persons insist on viewing science as only materialistic and the humanities as only idealistic, a fruitful collaboration of both fields is unlikely. The combination of science and the humanities is, however, possible, even probable, if we <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">begin by noting their common objectives</mark>, rather than seeing only their different means.</p>" },\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:555:        { kind: "reaction", html: "<p>After pointing out the shortcomings of both sides, the author continues with their comparison of the science and humanities. Noting their similarities and similar objectives, the author then goes on to reiterate the call to combine the two, emphasizing their similarities rather than differences.</p>" }\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\__tests__\\chapterMinutes.test.ts:37:    const reveal = s.reveal as { html: string } | undefined;\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\components\\TestDayOverlay.tsx:87:        <div className="td-task" dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: screen.task }} />\nsrc\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\components\\screens.tsx:16:const html = (value: string) => ({ __html: value });\nsrc\\hooks\\useTAChat.ts:19:  content_html: string;\nsrc\\hooks\\useStudentAssignments.ts:172:  content_html: string;\nsrc\\hooks\\useClassroomItems.ts:32:  content_html: string | null;\nsrc\\hooks\\useBookingScheduleItems.ts:26:        content_html: null,\nsrc\\lib\\agentActions.test.ts:104:      proposal({ kind: \'note\', resource: { title: \'Redo flaw\', kind: \'note\' }, generated_html: \'<p>do it</p>\' }),\nsrc\\lib\\assignmentEmail.ts:15: *   -
